Assay literature for SDC4 (syndecan‑4) in astrocyte–endothelial interaction and BBB biology

1) In vitro models interrogating SDC4 in barrier integrity and endothelial interaction

  • Brain microvascular endothelium under ischemia-like stress
  • Model: Human hCMEC/D3 and rat brain microvascular endothelial cells (rBMVECs) subjected to oxygen–glucose deprivation/reoxygenation (OGD/R); rat MCAO in vivo.
  • Manipulations: SDC4 overexpression vs interference (siRNA/shRNA); HOXB9 overexpression/interference with ChIP and dual‑luciferase confirmation of SDC4 promoter binding.
  • Readouts: TEER in vitro, tight‑junction IF (occludin/claudin‑5), Evans Blue extravasation in vivo; PKCα activation (biochemical readouts).
  • Findings: OGD/R upregulated SDC4; SDC4 overexpression reduced TEER and tight‑junction protein levels; SDC4 knockdown had opposite effects and limited Evans Blue leakage; HOXB9 drove SDC4 transcription and reproduced barrier loss via PKCα activation (PMID: 40571266).

  • Tier/liabilities: Functional BBB integrity; TEER is temperature- and electrode-sensitive; counterscreens: measure permeability and junctional proteins alongside TEER; include viability readouts.

  • Endothelial monolayers modeling permeability (glomerular ECs)

  • Model: Conditionally immortalized human glomerular endothelial cells (GEnCs) in Transwells; TNF‑α stimulation.
  • Manipulations: MMP inhibition (batimastat), MMP9 siRNA; SDC4 siRNA; enzymatic heparitinase; immunoprecipitation of shed ectodomains.
  • Readouts: Cell‑surface SDC4/HS (flow/dot blot), shed SDC4/HS (WB), albumin flux across monolayers.
  • Findings: TNF‑α induced SDC4 mRNA (+2.5×) but reduced cell‑surface SDC4 (−36%) and HS (−30%) with increased shedding (SDC4 +52% in medium); albumin passage increased by 37% and was blocked by batimastat or MMP9 siRNA; SDC4 siRNA reduced cell‑surface HS proportionally (PMID: 25122554).

  • Tier/liabilities: Functional permeability; albumin flux can be confounded by monolayer defects/cytotoxicity; counterscreens: TEER co‑measurement, LDH/viability, and microscopy for confluence.

  • Leukocyte adhesion/diapedesis on endothelium

  • Model: Human neutrophils adhering to HUVEC monolayers under static conditions with endotoxin/IL‑1 stimulation.
  • Manipulations: Antithrombin (AT) with/without heparin pentasaccharide; endothelial heparinase or chondroitinase pretreatment; anti‑SDC4 antibody.
  • Readouts: Static leukocyte adhesion counts; endothelial signaling by WB.
  • Findings: Endotoxin/IL‑1 increased adhesion; AT reduced adhesion via SDC4‑dependent signaling (heparin-binding site); heparinase/chondroitinase or anti‑SDC4 Ab abrogated AT’s effect and increased adhesion (PMID: 14652650).

  • Tier/liabilities: Functional adhesion; static assays lack shear; counterscreens: parallel‑plate flow adhesion where possible; isotype‑matched Abs and enzyme controls.

  • VE‑cadherin trafficking and junctional stability in endothelial cells

  • Model: Endothelial cells (e.g., HUVECs/BAOECs) analyzed for VE‑cadherin internalization and membrane levels.
  • Manipulations: SDC4 genetic perturbation; TMEM184A siRNA/overexpression with Sdc4 colocalization; HS‑dependence tested.
  • Readouts: Antibody‑feeding/internalization assays for VE‑cadherin; IF for junctional VE‑cad; migration (scratch‑wound); colocalization imaging.
  • Findings: SDC4 is required for VEGFA‑induced VE‑cadherin internalization in pathological angiogenesis (PMID: 33596666). TMEM184A–Sdc4 cooperation maintains VE‑cadherin levels; siTMEM decreased VE‑cad at membrane and increased migration; Sdc4 overexpression increased colocalization and trafficking (PMID: 40498012).

  • Tier/liabilities: Cell‑junction trafficking; fixation/internalization assay timing is failure‑prone; counterscreens: surface biotinylation, live‑cell imaging, and rescue constructs.

  • Mechanosensing in endothelial lineage cells (barrier gene program)

  • Model: Mouse ESC‑derived endothelial lineage cells under cyclic strain vs shear.
  • Manipulations: HS depletion, integrin blockade, Sdc4 siRNA.
  • Readouts: qPCR of EC markers (VE‑cadherin/PECAM‑1), tight‑junction genes (ZO‑1/OCLN/CLDN5), vasoactive genes.
  • Findings: Cyclic strain suppressed EC/junctional gene expression; HS depletion, integrin blockade, or Sdc4 silencing reversed suppression, identifying SDC4 as part of the mechanosensing complex (PMID: 31282995).

  • Tier/liabilities: Mechanistic gene readouts; not a barrier function per se; counterscreens: protein‑level validation and functional permeability.

  • Tumor–BBB interaction model (brain endothelium)

  • Model: Human brain microvascular endothelial cells (HBMEC) in an in vitro BBB transmigration assay with brain‑metastatic breast cancer cells.
  • Manipulations: Heparanase (HPSE) treatment; tumor cell SDC4/PKCα axis; GEF‑H1 knockdown in tumor cells; induction of VCAM1 in HBMECs.
  • Readouts: Transmigration across HBMEC monolayers; VCAM1 expression; RhoA/Rac1 activation.
  • Findings: HPSE modulated SDC4–PKCα signaling in tumor cells; GEF‑H1 KD reduced transmigration; HPSE increased HBMEC VCAM1; demonstrates SDC4‑linked cross‑talk affecting BBB interaction (PMID: 22513363).

  • Tier/liabilities: Heterotypic model; effects may be tumor‑cell intrinsic; counterscreens: endothelial‑specific manipulations and endothelial barrier measures (TEER/permeability).

  • Note on astrocyte–endothelial coculture BBB models specific to SDC4

  • Searches for direct astrocyte–endothelial transwells, microfluidic BBB chips, or 3D spheroids with astrocytic SDC4 perturbation found no reports (queries: “(syndecan-4 OR SDC4) AND astrocyte AND (endothelial OR BBB)”; “(syndecan-4 OR SDC4) AND (blood brain barrier OR endotheli) AND (transwell OR coculture OR microfluidic OR spheroid OR chip)”). Available astrocyte SDC4 studies address neuron–astrocyte signaling and astrocyte reactivity/migration rather than endothelial barrier measurements (PMIDs: 19723805, 21502139, 27235833, 28962574).

2) Mechanistic links from SDC4 to endothelial signaling, matrix remodeling and junctional control

  • VEGF/VE‑cadherin junctional dynamics (endothelial)
  • Assays: Antibody‑feeding/internalization of VE‑cadherin; imaging of adherens junctions; pathological angiogenesis models.

  • Findings: SDC4 is required for efficient VEGFA‑induced VE‑cadherin internalization, tying SDC4 to junction disassembly during angiogenic remodeling (PMID: 33596666). TMEM184A–Sdc4 colocalization supports VE‑cadherin membrane recovery (PMID: 40498012).

  • PKCα signaling

  • Assays: Biochemical activation of PKCα; genetic perturbation of SDC4 and transcriptional control by HOXB9 (ChIP/luciferase).
  • Findings: In brain endothelium subjected to OGD/R, SDC4 overexpression promoted PKCα activation; HOXB9 drove SDC4 transcription and reproduced barrier loss; SDC4 interference attenuated PKCα activation and preserved barrier (PMID: 40571266).

  • Astrocyte mechanistic bridge: Thy‑1 engagement of αvβ3 integrin and SDC4 in astrocytes required PKCα upstream of RhoA to form focal adhesions/migration (PMID: 19723805), with ATP‑P2X7‑Ca2+ signaling via hemichannels involved (PMIDs: 21502139, 27235833), highlighting a shared SDC4–PKCα axis in glia that may influence vascular interactions.

  • MMP activity and SDC4 shedding (glycocalyx remodeling)

  • Assays: qPCR/protein of SDC4 and HS; dot‑blot for HS; WB of shed ectodomain; albumin flux; MMP activity modulation (batimastat; MMP9 siRNA).

  • Quantitative effects: TNF‑α increased SDC4 mRNA 2.5×; decreased cell‑surface SDC4 36% and HS 30%; increased SDC4 shedding 52% and sulfated GAGs 65%; increased albumin permeability 37%; all abrogated by batimastat or MMP9 siRNA (PMID: 25122554). In diabetic kidney in vivo, MMP‑2/9 activities increased and an MMP‑2/9 inhibitor restored glycocalyx depth, reduced albuminuria, and reduced SDC4 shedding (PMID: 32037077).

  • Heparanase‑mediated remodeling and endothelial activation

  • Assays: HPSE treatment; GEF‑H1/RhoA/Rac1 activity assays; endothelial VCAM1 expression; tumor cell transmigration across HBMEC monolayers.

  • Findings: HPSE regulated the SDC4–PKCα axis in brain‑metastatic tumor cells; GEF‑H1 is a component of the SDC4 complex; HPSE increased HBMEC VCAM1; GEF‑H1 KD reduced BBB transmigration (PMID: 22513363).

  • Integrin activation and focal adhesion–junction cross‑talk (endothelial and astrocytic contexts)

  • Endothelial: Sdc4 knockdown decoupled vinculin from F‑actin, altered actin, and produced VE‑cadherin‑rich, unstable junctions; reduced adhesion, increased migration, and impaired tube formation (PMID: 28062282).

  • Astrocytes: Thy‑1 engages αvβ3 integrin + SDC4 to activate PKCα→RhoA; SDC4 silencing or cytoplasmic tail truncation blocked adhesion/migration responses (PMID: 19723805).

  • Viral uptake at the BBB interface

  • Assays: Overexpression/knockdown of syndecans; AAV9 transduction; co‑IP and affinity proteomics.
  • Findings: SDC4 facilitated AAV9 cellular entry beyond HS chain binding, implicating SDC4 core protein interactions; SDC4 overexpression enabled entry in poorly transducible cells, while knockdown reduced entry (PMID: 36834552).

3) Neurodegeneration/AD‑relevant systems and outcomes

  • AD‑specific evidence (biomarker/associational, not functional barrier assays)
  • CSF SDC4: Elevated in preclinical AD; higher CSF SDC4 correlated with higher amyloid‑PET and tau‑PET, worse baseline cognition, and predicted faster amyloid/tau accumulation and cognitive decline over ~8 years (Olink PEA platform), placing SDC4 upregulation near amyloid‑positivity with further rise post tau‑positivity (PMID: 42098850).

  • AD brain tissue: SDC4 overexpressed in multiple regions; IHC localized SDC4 with tau and Aβ pathology; mRNA profiling across 17 HSPG core proteins highlighted SDC4 upregulation (PMID: 32232475).

  • BBB/neurovascular evidence in CNS injury and ischemia (indirect to AD)

  • Endothelial HOXB9–SDC4 axis: In MCAO rats and OGD/R brain ECs, SDC4 overexpression reduced TEER/tight junctions and increased Evans Blue leakage; SDC4 interference protected BBB; effects mediated by PKCα (PMID: 40571266).
  • Microglial SDC4: AAV‑shSDC4 improved neurological deficits, restored microglial mitochondrial morphology/function, increased occludin/claudin‑5, and protected BBB after I/R; mechanistically, microglial SDC4 interacted with Dishevelled to inhibit Wnt/β‑catenin signaling; Wnt activation mediated protection (PMID: 42215496).
  • Note: No reports located of Aβ challenge or AD mouse–derived astrocytes/endothelium directly perturbed for SDC4 in BBB models with TEER or permeability readouts (PubMed queries above).

4) Assay artifacts and counterscreens reported for SDC4 studies

  • Redundancy among HSPGs and HS dependency
  • Artifact: Heparinase/HS‑mimetics (heparin, fucoidan) affect all HS proteoglycans and many ligands, risking nonspecific effects on chemokines, growth factors, and integrins.

  • Counterscreens used: Isoform‑specific SDC4 siRNA and rescue with signaling‑competent/deficient mutants (astrocyte studies used cytoplasmic‑tail truncations to separate signaling from binding; PMIDs: 19723805, 27235833). Parallel measurement of SDC1/2/3 expression to detect compensatory changes (differential syndecan regulation documented in retinal endothelium; PMID: 34801534). Endothelial context documented SDC4 as predominant HS proteoglycan in GEnC glycocalyx (PMID: 25122554).

  • MMP inhibition specificity

  • Artifact: Broad‑spectrum MMP inhibitors (e.g., batimastat) have off‑targets and can alter barrier via multiple substrates.

  • Counterscreens used: Pair pharmacologic MMP blockade with MMP9 siRNA to confirm enzyme specificity (both abrogated TNF‑α‑induced SDC4 shedding and albumin flux; PMID: 25122554). Direct assays of MMP‑2/9 activity (gelatin zymography/biochemical activity in tissue and plasma) in vivo with inhibitor reversal (PMID: 32037077).

  • Antibody epitope/agonism issues

  • Artifact: Anti‑SDC4 antibodies can perturb adhesion signaling (agonist/antagonist or Fc‑mediated effects).

  • Counterscreens used: Heparinase/chondroitinase digestion and AT heparin‑binding competition to map the SDC4‑dependent effect (PMID: 14652650). Recommend multiple antibody clones, Fab fragments, and genetic KD/KO corroboration.

  • TEER and permeability assay sensitivity

  • Artifact: TEER is highly sensitive to temperature, media conductivity, and electrode fouling; FITC‑dextran leakage may reflect endocytosis or filter defects; albumin adsorption to plastic can skew flux.

  • Counterscreens used: Combining TEER with paracellular tracer permeability (dextran/albumin), immunostaining and immunoblotting for claudin‑5/occludin/ZO‑1, and viability assays (PMIDs: 40571266, 25122554). Include monolayer confluence imaging.

  • Static leukocyte adhesion vs physiological flow

  • Artifact: Static adhesion overestimates interactions that are shear‑dependent; rolling and diapedesis dynamics differ.

  • Counterscreens: Where feasible, confirm under flow (not reported in 14652650). Use selectin/integrin blocking as specificity controls (14652650 leveraged GAG enzymolysis and AT‑pentasaccharide competition).

  • Genetic perturbation off‑targets

  • Artifact: siRNA/CRISPR off‑target gene modulation can confound barrier phenotypes.

  • Counterscreens used: Multiple independent siRNAs and rescue with siRNA‑resistant cDNA (astrocyte SDC4 pathway studies used silencing plus signaling‑dead mutants; PMIDs: 19723805, 27235833). In endothelium, paired transcriptional control (HOXB9 ChIP/luciferase) with SDC4 interference strengthened causal assignment (PMID: 40571266).

  • Cell type and species differences

  • Artifact: Endothelial subtype (brain vs glomerular vs aortic) and species (human/rat/mouse/bovine) alter glycocalyx composition and SDC4 predominance.

  • Counterscreens: Replicate findings across brain‑relevant endothelium (hCMEC/D3/rBMVEC) and, where applicable, report SDC4 isoform predominance (PMIDs: 40571266, 25122554).

  • Readout tiering: target engagement vs function vs phenotype

  • Engagement: SDC4 binding/colocalization (e.g., AAV9 entry via SDC4; co‑IP/proteomics; PMID: 36834552).
  • Functional: Barrier TEER/permeability, leukocyte adhesion, junctional internalization (PMIDs: 40571266, 25122554, 14652650, 33596666).
  • Phenotype: Ischemia‑induced BBB disruption and behavioral outcomes; AD CSF biomarker trajectories (PMIDs: 40571266, 42215496, 42098850).
  • Bridge with orthogonal assays to confirm that target engagement (SDC4 modulation, shedding) produces functional barrier changes and relevant in vivo phenotypes.

References (selected)

  • Endothelial BBB ischemia models and HOXB9–SDC4–PKCα axis: (PMID: 40571266)
  • MMP9‑mediated SDC4 shedding and permeability in endothelial monolayers: (PMID: 25122554); in vivo MMP‑2/9 inhibition restoring glycocalyx: (PMID: 32037077)
  • VEGFA‑induced VE‑cadherin internalization requires SDC4: (PMID: 33596666)
  • TMEM184A–Sdc4 cooperation maintains VE‑cadherin levels: (PMID: 40498012)
  • Sdc4 knockdown alters endothelial focal adhesions and VE‑cadherin‑rich junctions: (PMID: 28062282)
  • Antithrombin anti‑adhesive effect via endothelial SDC4; leukocyte adhesion assays: (PMID: 14652650)
  • SDC4 mechanosensing in endothelial lineage under cyclic strain: (PMID: 31282995)
  • Heparanase regulation of SDC4/PKCα; HBMEC VCAM1 and BBB transmigration: (PMID: 22513363)
  • Astrocyte SDC4 with αvβ3 integrin activates PKCα→RhoA; adhesion/migration; ATP–P2X7 signaling: (PMIDs: 19723805, 21502139, 27235833, 28962574)
  • AAV9 cellular entry facilitated by SDC4: (PMID: 36834552)
  • AD‑relevant SDC4: CSF SDC4 predicts amyloid/tau and cognition (PMID: 42098850); SDC4 overexpressed and plaque/tangle‑associated in AD brains (PMID: 32232475)
Medicinal Chemistry & Chemical Biology — full specialist report

Task: From literature and established resources (including Open Targets tractability), answer: 1) What therapeutic modalities have precedent for targeting SDC4 or closely related syndecans: small molecules disrupting cytoplasmic interactions (e.g., PKCα, PIP2, syntenin/PDZ), peptide mimetics, antibodies/biologics against ectodomains, or HS mimetics/heparanase modulators that functionally modulate SDC4 pathways? List specific compounds/peptides/antibodies with potency, selectivity, and cellular activity where reported. 2) Are there high-quality chemical probes/tool compounds for SDC4-driven pathways (criteria: potency <1 µM, demonstrated on-target mechanism, selectivity, and cellular activity)? Include any caveats about off-targets and assay interference. 3) Tractability and developability: Summarize Open Targets tractability for SDC4 (small molecule/antibody), and discuss delivery considerations for acting at astrocyte–endothelial interfaces in the brain (e.g., BBB access for intracellular modulators vs. abluminal exposure for biologics), referencing published PK/brain-penetration data for analogous agents where available. 4) Safety/target liability: Based on expression breadth (from public atlases) and known biology of SDC4 in vasculature, wound healing, and immunity, what on-target risks are reported or anticipated (e.g., bleeding/angiogenesis, fibrosis, impaired repair)? Include any clinical or advanced preclinical agents modulating syndecan pathways and their safety signals. Focus on documented agents and tractability assessments. Do not present new screening plans or assay design; do not restate expression/genetic evidence except as context for safety and modality choice. · 27 PubMed calls

Modalities with precedent for targeting SDC4/syndecans and the HS/heparanase axis

Small molecules that disrupt cytoplasmic partners (PDZ/SDCBP-syntenin)
- PDZ1i (also reported as 113B7): a fragment-derived small molecule that binds the PDZ1 domain of syntenin (SDCBP), the canonical PDZ partner of syndecans. Biophysical affinity for MDA‑9/Syntenin PDZ1: Kd ≈ 21 µM; negligible binding to PDZ2 (in vitro NMR/FP) (PMID: 36834839). In vivo, PDZ1i was “well-tolerated,” showed t1/2 ≈ 9 h, and suppressed prostate cancer pathogenesis (Mol Cancer Ther) (PMID: 31345950). PDZ1i inhibited invasion/metastasis and radiosensitized glioblastoma models (PNAS) (PMID: 28011764) and reduced breast cancer metastasis via IL‑1β/STAT3 pathway modulation (PNAS) (PMID: 34016751). Although these data establish tractability of syntenin–PDZ1, potency remains micromolar and selectivity across the broader PDZome is incompletely defined.
- Newer syntenin PDZ inhibitors: fragment/NMR-derived dual-PDZ hits (PI1A, PI1B; PI2A, PI2B) that disrupt PDZ1/PDZ2 interactions and suppress tumor-cell migration; structures and binding modes reported, but potencies are still low- to mid‑micromolar by competitive FP and not yet optimized for drug-like affinity (PMID: 36834839). A follow-on, improved scaffold (IVMT‑Rx‑4) derived from the PDZ1i series reported better solubility and lower efflux, with anti-metastatic activity in prostate cancer bone metastasis models and combination benefit with docetaxel (PMID: 41865851).

Peptide mimetics that disrupt SDC ectodomain complexes (“synstatins”)
- SDC4-derived synstatin (SSTN87–131): peptide from the SDC4 ectodomain that directly and competitively displaces EGFR family members and α3β1 integrin from SDC4; displacement IC50 ≈ 100–300 nM in purified-protein assays. It blocks EGFR capture by SDC4 and inhibits α6β4‑integrin–dependent epithelial cell motility (J Biol Chem) (PMID: 26350464).
- SDC1-derived synstatins: original SSTN peptide disrupts SDC1–αvβ3/αvβ5 complexes and blocks angiogenesis in vitro and in vivo; systemic administration impaired tumor growth in orthotopic mammary models (J Exp Med) (PMID: 19255147). A cancer-focused IGF1R‑complex synstatin (SSTNIGF1R) inactivated IGF1R and reduced HNSCC PDX growth by 85–95% with biomarker evidence of complex disruption (Cancer Res Commun) (PMID: 36968227). While SDC1/SDC4 synstatins target different receptor captures, both exemplify tractable, high‑nM peptide PPI disruptors of syndecan ectodomain assemblies.

Antibodies/biologics to SDC4 ectodomain
- Anti‑SDC4 dimerization‑blocking monoclonal antibody: engagement of SDC4 by IL‑1 drives SDC4 dimerization and IL‑1R1 trafficking; a monoclonal antibody directed against the SDC4 dimerization domain reduced IL‑1R1 surface expression, ERK signaling and MMP‑3 production in fibroblasts, and decreased histologic paw destruction in a TNFα‑transgenic arthritis model (Ann Rheum Dis) (PMID: 32094158). This shows feasibility of function‑blocking anti‑SDC4 antibodies in vivo.

Heparan sulfate (HS) mimetics and heparanase (HPSE) modulators that functionally modulate syndecan pathways
- PI‑88 (muparfostat): a sulfated oligosaccharide HS mimetic that inhibits heparanase and HS-binding growth factors. As adjuvant therapy after HCC resection, 160 mg/day improved recurrence‑free rate (50%→63%) and delayed recurrence; 250 mg/day had more hepatotoxicity-related withdrawals (phase II; J Hepatol and long‑term follow‑up) (PMIDs: 19303160, 25170226). In combination with docetaxel in CRPC, the trial was stopped early due to high febrile neutropenia (27%), but showed activity (Ann Oncol) (PMID: 19917571).
- Roneparstat (SST0001): glycol-split heparin heparanase inhibitor; extensive preclinical activity and a phase I program in multiple myeloma reviewed (Adv Exp Med Biol) (PMID: 32274725).
- Pixatimod (PG545): lipophilic HS mimetic with heparanase and growth factor inhibition; discovery and preclinical PK/PD reported (J Med Chem) (PMID: 22458531). Early clinical experience and biomarker modulation (Eur J Cancer; J Immunother Cancer) (PMIDs: 25754234, 29898788). Preclinically, PG545 interfered with Wnt/β‑catenin and suppressed pancreatic tumorigenesis (Oncotarget) (PMID: 25669977).
- Mechanistic link to vascular endothelial activation: heparanase enhances syndecan‑1 shedding, and shed SDC1–HS captures VEGF to ECM and drives endothelial invasion; synstatin peptide blocks this complex and endothelial invasion (Blood) (PMID: 20097882). This illustrates how heparanase/HS mimetics can indirectly modulate syndecan‑driven endothelial responses.

Notes on measured activities recorded in ChEMBL for “SDC4”
- ChEMBL lists two “SDC4‑ROS1” Ba/F3 cell viability assays with approved TKIs: brigatinib IC50 16 nM and crizotinib IC50 17 nM (PMID: 27780853). These values reflect inhibition of the ROS1 kinase in an SDC4‑ROS1 fusion context, not modulation of SDC4 itself; they are not SDC4‑directed chemical matter.

Relevance to astrocyte–endothelial interface in brain
- SDC4 on astrocytes cooperates with αvβ3 integrin upon neuronal Thy‑1 engagement to activate PKCα and RhoA, promoting focal adhesion/stress fibers in astrocytes (J Cell Sci) (PMID: 19723805). In endothelium, SDC4 is required for VEGFA‑induced VE‑cadherin internalization (a key step in permeability/angiogenesis) (ATVB) (PMID: 33596666) and for vinculin–F‑actin coupling with consequences for endothelial behavior (Matrix Biol) (PMID: 28062282). Together these data support a mechanism wherein SDC4 modulates astrocyte adhesion/signaling and endothelial junctional dynamics, potentially relevant to BBB dysfunction in AD neuroinflammation.

Tool compounds / chemical-probe quality

  • No high-quality chemical probe is registered for SDC4 in the Chemical Probes Portal/SGC (i.e., none independently rated for selectivity and on‑target mechanism) (Chemical Probes tool).
  • Syntenin PDZ inhibitors (PDZ1i; newer dual-PDZ hits) are useful pathway tools but currently have modest affinity (PDZ1i Kd ~21 µM) and incomplete PDZome selectivity data; they meet cellular activity and in vivo efficacy in oncology models but do not meet a sub‑µM probe criterion (PMIDs: 31345950, 28011764, 34016751, 36834839).
  • Synstatin peptides (SSTN87–131 for SDC4; SSTN variants for SDC1) have clear, direct mechanisms (disrupt defined ectodomain capture sites) with 100–300 nM displacement potencies against purified complexes and reproducible cell/in vivo phenotypes (PMIDs: 26350464, 19255147, 36968227). Caveats: as peptides, they have developability limits, can engage multiple receptor complexes organized by the same syndecan, and require matched negative controls to exclude off‑target adhesion effects.
  • Heparanase/HS mimetics (PI‑88, roneparstat, pixatimod) modulate syndecan pathways but are pleiotropic poly‑anions that bind many HS‑binding proteins and can exert anticoagulant effects; interpret cellular phenotypes with these agents cautiously for target attribution. Clinical safety signals include cytopenias, PT prolongation, hepatotoxicity at higher PI‑88 dosing, and myelosuppression when combined with cytotoxics (PMIDs: 19303160, 19917571, 25170226).
  • ChEMBL entries for “SDC4‑ROS1” (brigatinib, crizotinib) capture kinase inhibition in a ROS1 fusion and are not SDC4 tools (PMID: 27780853).

Tractability and developability (including Open Targets-like considerations) and delivery to astrocyte–endothelial interfaces

  • Antibody/biologic tractability: The SDC4 ectodomain is accessible, and function‑blocking antibodies (anti‑dimerization) demonstrated in vivo activity in inflammation (PMID: 32094158). General experience with syndecan‑targeting biologics (e.g., anti‑SDC1 ADCs in myeloma) confirms the family’s druggability at the cell surface (phase 1/2 ADC combinations; Lancet Haematol) (PMID: 34529955). For CNS, however, IgG brain exposure after systemic dosing is low (well‑established textbook background); effective action at the abluminal (brain) side of the BBB would likely require intrathecal/intracerebroventricular delivery or BBB shuttles (e.g., receptor‑mediated transcytosis constructs; general background).
  • Small‑molecule tractability: The SDC4 ectodomain mediates protein–protein captures; classical small molecules for the ectodomain are unlikely. The intracellular tail’s PPIs (syntenin–PDZ, PIP2/PKCα) provide tractable PPI pockets; PDZ1 is shown druggable but with modest current potencies (PMIDs: 31345950, 36834839). Achieving BBB penetration for intracellular modulators will require CNS‑drug‑like properties; PDZ1i showed efficacy in an orthotopic GBM context (PMID: 28011764), which suggests at least tumor‑brain exposure, though dedicated brain PK was not reported in the abstract and tumor BBB may be compromised.
  • Peptide modulators: Synstatins have sub‑µM potencies in displacement assays and in vivo activity outside CNS (angiogenesis/tumor models) (PMIDs: 19255147, 26350464, 36968227). As mid‑size, highly polar peptides, passive BBB penetration is unlikely; CNS delivery would require local administration or shuttle platforms (textbook background).
  • HS mimetics/heparanase inhibitors: Large, highly charged molecules—systemically active on vasculature/ECM; BBB penetration is expected to be poor (textbook background). Nonetheless, they can modulate endothelial permeability/angiogenesis peripherally and could, in principle, impact BBB leakiness indirectly (PMID: 20097882).
  • Predicted chemical matter: DrugCLIP returned no predicted binders for SDC4, indicating no screened high‑confidence pocket in that resource; this is a tractability gap for de novo small molecules (DrugCLIP tool).
  • Measured chemical matter in ChEMBL: limited to ROS1‑fusion cell assays (not SDC4 ligands). Net: there is no published, potent, selective, brain‑penetrant small‑molecule SDC4 modulator; the most concrete tractable levers are function‑blocking antibodies and synstatin‑class peptides, with syntenin‑PDZ small molecules as an emerging but still weak‑affinity pathway approach.

Safety/target liability (on‑target risks anticipated from vascular, wound-healing and immune biology)

  • Endothelial junctions and permeability: SDC4 is required for VEGFA‑induced VE‑cadherin internalization; inhibiting SDC4 function can stabilize junctions and reduce pathological angiogenesis/permeability (ATVB) (PMID: 33596666). Potential benefit in diseases with BBB leak; conversely, risk of impairing physiological angiogenesis and vascular remodeling.
  • Endothelial cytoskeleton/adhesion: SDC4 knockdown perturbs vinculin–F‑actin coupling and endothelial behavior (Matrix Biol) (PMID: 28062282), consistent with possible effects on barrier mechanics.
  • Wound healing/angiogenesis: SDC4‑null mice are viable but display impaired wound healing and angiogenesis; fibroblasts show adhesion/migration defects (Curr Opin Cell Biol; commentary summarizing two KO lines) (PMID: 11544026). On‑target inhibition could delay tissue repair.
  • Platelets/bleeding: Human platelets express SDC4; ligation by antithrombin modulates platelet‑dependent neutrophil superoxide release (Thromb Haemost) (PMID: 15968398). While not direct evidence of a bleeding phenotype, SDC4 perturbation may interact with hemostatic signaling.
  • Leukocyte trafficking: SDC4 participates in leukocyte adhesion/extravasation; SDC4‑dependent eosinophil migration was reported (Clin Exp Allergy) (PMID: 15144459). Anti‑SDC4 interventions could alter inflammatory cell recruitment.
  • Innate cytokine signaling: Anti‑SDC4 dimerization antibody reduced IL‑1R1 trafficking/expression and downstream signaling in fibrotic synoviocytes and reduced arthritis damage (PMID: 32094158). In the CNS, attenuating SDC4‑facilitated cytokine responsiveness in astrocytes might reduce neuroinflammation, but could also blunt host defense (extrapolation; mechanism‑based).
  • Heparanase/HS mimetics safety signals:
  • PI‑88 160 mg/day was optimal/safe in adjuvant HCC (hepatotoxicity/washouts concentrated at 250 mg/day), with AEs including cytopenia, injection‑site hemorrhage, and PT prolongation (PMID: 19303160; follow‑up: PMID: 25170226).
  • In combination with docetaxel, febrile neutropenia was 27% leading to early stop (PMID: 19917571).
  • Roneparstat and pixatimod have early‑phase clinical exposure; pixatimod has immunostimulatory TLR9 activity in addition to heparanase inhibition (PMIDs: 32274725, 25754234, 29898788). These profiles flag bleeding/coagulation and myelosuppression as class risks for HS mimetics; hepatotoxicity is dose‑limiting for PI‑88.

Implications for an AD vascular–glial strategy
- Mechanistic plausibility: Astrocyte SDC4 integrates Thy‑1/αvβ3 signals to PKCα/RhoA, affecting adhesion (PMID: 19723805), while endothelial SDC4 drives VEGFA‑induced VE‑cadherin internalization (PMID: 33596666). In AD, where IL‑1/VEGF and BBB leak are prominent, SDC4 function‑blocking at the astrocyte–endothelial interface could, in principle, reduce pro‑inflammatory receptor trafficking and stabilize endothelial junctions.
- Modality choice:
- Antibodies against SDC4 ectodomain (anti‑dimerization) are directly justified by in vivo data (PMID: 32094158) but will face BBB delivery constraints (textbook background).
- Synstatin‑type peptides targeting SDC4 ectodomain capture (SSTN87‑131) are potent in PPI displacement (100–300 nM) (PMID: 26350464) but are unlikely to cross the BBB without a shuttle (textbook background).
- Intracellular PDZ inhibitors of syntenin provide a drug-like route to modulate SDC4 signaling complexes but currently lack potency/selectivity and have unproven BBB exposure as a class (PMIDs: 31345950, 28011764, 36834839).
- Overall tractability: Extracellular function‑blocking modalities (antibody/peptide) are the clearest, mechanism‑anchored levers for SDC4; intracellular small molecules are emergent and need potency/ADME optimization, especially for CNS. No predicted‑binder hits (DrugCLIP) and the absence of bona fide SDC4 small‑molecule ligands in ChEMBL reinforce the need for modality‑led strategies.

Key caveats and guidance for tool use

  • There is no independently endorsed chemical probe for SDC4 (Chemical Probes tool). Studies using PDZ1i or synstatins should include orthogonal genetic controls to anchor on‑target mechanism. HS mimetics/heparanase inhibitors are pleiotropic; off‑target anticoagulant/immunomodulatory effects must be controlled for.
  • ChEMBL “SDC4‑ROS1” entries (brigatinib/crizotinib) reflect ROS1 kinase inhibition in an SDC4‑ROS1 fusion model (PMID: 27780853) and should not be used as SDC4 tools.

References (selected)

  • Anti‑SDC4 dimerization antibody reduces IL‑1R trafficking/signaling and arthritis damage (PMID: 32094158).
  • SDC4–EGFR/α6β4 capture; SDC4 synstatin SSTN87–131 displaces with IC50 100–300 nM (PMID: 26350464).
  • SDC1 synstatin blocks angiogenesis and tumor growth (PMID: 19255147); SSTNIGF1R suppresses HNSCC PDX growth (PMID: 36968227).
  • Astrocyte SDC4 with αvβ3 activates PKCα/RhoA upon Thy‑1 engagement (PMID: 19723805).
  • Endothelial SDC4 required for VEGFA‑induced VE‑cadherin internalization (PMID: 33596666); vinculin–F‑actin coupling requires SDC4 (PMID: 28062282).
  • Syntenin PDZ1 inhibitor PDZ1i: in vivo t1/2 ~9 h; anti‑metastatic and radiosensitizing activity (PMIDs: 31345950, 28011764, 34016751). Newer dual‑PDZ hits and binding modes (PMID: 36834839). Improved analog IVMT‑Rx‑4 (PMID: 41865851).
  • Heparanase/HS mimetics: PI‑88 phase II adjuvant HCC benefit at 160 mg/day; hepatotoxicity at 250 mg/day (PMIDs: 19303160, 25170226); combo myelosuppression (PMID: 19917571). Roneparstat review (PMID: 32274725). Pixatimod PG545 discovery and early clinical (PMIDs: 22458531, 25754234, 29898788). Heparanase‑driven SDC1 shedding drives endothelial invasion; synstatin blocks (PMID: 20097882).
  • SDC4 KO wound‑healing/angiogenesis defects (PMID: 11544026). Platelet SDC4 (PMID: 15968398). Eosinophil migration requires SDC4 (PMID: 15144459).
  • ChEMBL “SDC4‑ROS1” fusion assays with brigatinib/crizotinib (PMID: 27780853).

Methodological notes
- Predicted chemical matter: DrugCLIP had no entry for SDC4 (gap).
- Measured bioactivity: ChEMBL returned only ROS1‑fusion cellular viability assays, not SDC4 ligands.
- Statements about antibody BBB exposure and peptide brain penetration are standard background without a specific citation (textbook pharmacology).

New: snRNA‑seq Alzheimer’s vs normal — SDC4 in astrocyte and vascular cells (tool‑mandated CELLxGENE scan)

What changed
- The mandated singlecell_disease_expression tool provides pooled, descriptive Alzheimer’s vs normal metrics across aggregated snRNA‑seq datasets, not per‑cohort differential expression with log2FC and FDR. Therefore, no per‑cohort log2FC/FDR, donor counts, or brain region breakdown can be reported from this source. Directional differences below are leads only, not tested effects. This limits our ability to claim significance or cross‑cohort concordance for endothelial/pericyte signals at single‑cell resolution.

Pooled descriptive metrics (brain; AD‑labeled vs normal‑labeled cells; fraction expressing, mean among expressing cells, and cell counts)
- Astrocyte
- Astrocyte: AD 26.4% expressing, mean 2.06, n=9,993; normal 13.4%, mean 1.84, n=140,244 (CZ CELLxGENE Discover; singlecell_disease_expression tool, this run).
- Mature astrocyte: AD 35.1%, mean 2.27, n=3,143; normal 20.4%, mean 2.74, n=450.
- Vascular cells
- Endothelial cell: AD 0.8%, mean 2.10, n=43; normal 0.6%, mean 1.78, n=815.
- Pericyte (perivascular cell): AD 2.5%, mean 1.80, n=38; normal 0.6%, mean 1.71, n=194.
- Smooth muscle cell: AD 1.6%, mean 1.96, n=34; normal 1.4%, mean 1.83, n=151.
- Contractile cell (lineage‑lumped category including vascular SMC/pericyte states): AD 2.0%, mean 1.88, n=72; normal 0.8%, mean 1.77, n=345.

What is not available from the tool (hence cannot be reported here)
- Per‑cohort log2FC and adjusted p/FDR, donor counts, and brain region(s) for each cohort/cell type.
- Statistical tests of AD vs normal at single‑cell resolution; cohort‑level concordance/discordance.

Interpretation for the mechanism question
- Astrocytes: Across aggregated datasets, SDC4 is detected in a larger fraction of AD‑labeled astrocytes (including mature astrocytes). Per‑cell mean among expressors is modestly higher in “astrocyte” and lower in “mature astrocyte,” suggesting state/composition shifts rather than a uniform per‑cell upregulation. Without per‑cohort DE testing, these remain descriptive.
- Endothelial/pericyte: SDC4 detection is low but present, with slightly higher AD‑labeled fractions and means. However, AD‑labeled vascular cell counts are very small (endothelial n=43; pericyte n=38), precluding any inference about significance or robust induction under AD from this tool.
- Net effect on conclusions: These pooled single‑cell summaries weakly support the astrocyte‑centric view (more astrocytes expressing SDC4 in AD labeling) and do not provide statistically tested evidence for endothelial or pericyte upregulation. They neither strengthen nor refute endothelial/pericyte involvement mechanistically; they simply remain underpowered and untested for significance at single‑cell resolution.

Source
- CZ CELLxGENE Discover, singlecell_disease_expression tool (this run) for SDC4 in “brain” with disease “Alzheimer disease.” The tool aggregates independently collected snRNA‑seq datasets; disease and normal are different donors/studies with no matching and no statistical test, so differences are leads only.

Bioinformatician — follow-up 2 — full specialist report

Task: Question: What is SDC4’s human loss‑of‑function tolerance to inform safety for antagonistic modalities? - Use gnomad_constraint to retrieve LOEUF, pLI, observed:expected LoF (and 90% CI), observed and expected LoF counts, missense Z‑score, and synonymous Z‑score for SDC4. - State any flags for regional constraint if available. - Provide a short interpretation for systemic vs CNS‑restricted inhibition risk. Tool to use: gnomad_constraint(SDC4). · 1 PubMed call

Human LoF tolerance for SDC4 — what changed

Finding: SDC4 is LoF‑tolerant in humans (LOEUF 1.245), which is reassuring for antagonistic modalities. This reduces systemic on‑target toxicity concern in the report’s risk assessment; it does not bear on efficacy.

  • Source: gnomAD v4 constraint, ENSG00000124145.
  • LOEUF (upper bound of o/e LoF): 1.245.
  • o/e LoF (point estimate, 90% CI): 0.830 (0.568–1.245); observed vs expected LoF variants: 17 / 20.5.
  • pLI: 0.000 (report LOEUF for interpretation).
  • Missense: o/e 0.955.
  • Synonymous: o/e 1.016.
  • Missense Z‑score and synonymous Z‑score: not provided by this tool output (o/e reported instead).
  • Regional/subgenic constraint flags: none returned.

Interpretation for antagonism (safety lens only; human genetic tier)
- Systemic inhibition: Human germline data indicate SDC4 LoF is tolerated (LOEUF ≥1), suggesting a lower risk of systemic on‑target toxicity from partial pharmacologic antagonism. Caveat: lifetime heterozygous LoF ≠ adult dosing; acute pathway‑specific liabilities are not captured by population selection.
- CNS‑restricted inhibition: Expected risk is at least as low as systemic and likely lower, given LoF tolerance and reduced peripheral exposure with CNS‑targeted delivery. This remains a tolerability statement only; it does not inform efficacy.

Citation: gnomAD v4 constraint (ENSG00000124145); values as listed above.

Assay Development & HTS — follow-up 2 — full specialist report

Task: Question: Do any astrocyte–endothelial co‑culture, tri‑culture, spheroid, or BBB‑on‑chip studies directly perturb SDC4 (genetic or pharmacologic) and report TEER and/or tracer permeability, ideally under AD‑relevant challenges (Aβ, inflammatory cytokines, VEGFA)? - Perform a targeted literature search and return any qualifying studies with: model type, species/cell sources, perturbation (KO/KD/OE, blocking Ab/peptide), challenges, readouts (TEER, permeability, VE‑cadherin dynamics, glycocalyx), and quantitative effect sizes with statistics. - If no studies exist, state this explicitly and list the closest near‑misses (e.g., endothelial‑only with SDC4 perturbation; astrocyte‑only without barrier readouts). Tool to use: consensus_search. · 3 PubMed calls

Gap closure: no co-culture/BBB‑on‑chip study has directly perturbed SDC4 and measured TEER/permeability (confirmed absence)

  • Targeted full‑text retrieval did not identify any astrocyte–endothelial co‑culture, tri‑culture, spheroid, or BBB‑on‑chip study that genetically or pharmacologically perturbed SDC4 and reported TEER and/or tracer permeability under AD‑relevant challenges (Aβ, inflammatory cytokines, VEGFA). This confirms the report’s stated gap: direct, causal testing of SDC4 in astrocyte–endothelial BBB systems with barrier readouts remains unpublished.

Closest near‑misses (model has co‑/tri‑culture BBB with TEER/permeability and AD‑relevant challenges, but no SDC4 perturbation)

  • Human BBB‑on‑a‑chip with TEER and sodium fluorescein permeability; neuroinflammation challenge
  • Model: Human BBB‑on‑chip; HBMEC vessels with TEER electrodes; co‑exposed to TNFα+IL‑1β.
  • Readouts: TEER (baseline 20–22 Ω·cm2); sodium fluorescein Papp 2.6 ± 1.2 × 10−6 cm/s at baseline; high cytokines (3.3–10 ng/mL) significantly increase leakage; TEER most sensitive (one‑way ANOVA/Kruskal–Wallis, multiple p thresholds significant) (PMID: 37818458; full‑text excerpts).
  • AD relevance: pro‑inflammatory cytokines.

  • SDC4: not perturbed.

  • BBB‑on‑chip with integrated micro‑TEER; human tri‑culture (endothelium + astrocytes + pericytes)

  • Model: 3D ECM scaffold with human ECs, astrocytes, pericytes; micro‑TEER integrated.
  • Readouts: TEER increased from ~5,400 to 12,480 Ω·cm2 from day 4 to day 10, indicating barrier maturation; permeability reduced correspondingly (PMID: 36978078; full‑text excerpts).

  • AD relevance: device used to evaluate nanotherapeutics targeted to AD; no SDC4 perturbation.

  • Microfluidic µSiM‑BBB with astrocyte co‑culture; systemic inflammation challenge

  • Model: Human EECM‑BMEC with primary human astrocytes in µSiM‑BBB under shear.
  • Readouts: Permeability significantly increased with high‑dose “cytomix” (e.g., 50 pg/mL; one‑way ANOVA with Tukey, p < 0.01 to **p < 0.0001); adding fibrinogen triggered robust astrocyte activation without further permeability increase vs cytomix alone (PMID: 40867058; full‑text excerpts).

  • AD relevance: inflammatory challenge; SDC4 not perturbed.

  • Astrocyte–endothelial co‑culture (static transwells); AD model astrocytes impair barrier support

  • Model: hCMEC/D3 endothelial monolayers co‑cultured with immortalized astrocytes from 3xTG‑AD or WT mice.
  • Readouts: WT astrocytes increased endothelial tight‑junction protein expression and TEER; 3xTG‑AD astrocytes failed to increase TJ proteins or TEER (PMID: 32440709; full‑text excerpts).

  • AD relevance: AD astrocytes; SDC4 not perturbed.

  • Primate BBB co‑culture (monkey ECs + rat astrocytes/pericytes)

  • Readouts: TEER rose to 545 ± 40 Ω·cm2 (day 4) in tri‑culture; fluorescein permeability decreased; junctional proteins upregulated (PMID: 34575559; full‑text excerpts).

  • SDC4 not perturbed.

  • Platform effects of astrocyte co‑culture on hiPSC‑BMECs (insert vs chip; 2D vs 3D astrocytes)

  • Readouts: 2D astrocyte co‑culture increased permeability; switching astrocytes to 3D Matrigel reduced permeability; multiple tracer Papp quantified (Lucifer yellow, 4/20 kDa dextran, [3H]mannitol) (PMID: 40524169; full‑text excerpts).
  • SDC4 not perturbed.

Near‑misses with direct SDC4 perturbation but lacking astrocyte co‑culture

  • Brain endothelial monolayers; ischemia‑like OGD/R with SDC4 knockdown
  • Model: hCMEC/D3 ± si‑SDC4; OGD/R.
  • Readouts: SDC4 interference attenuated OGD/R‑induced TEER loss; preserved ZO‑1/claudin‑5/occludin (immunofluorescence and Western); in vivo MCAO: SDC4 interference reduced BBB disruption (PMID: 40571266 / Brain Res Bull 2025 doi:10.1016/j.brainresbull.2025.111441; full‑text excerpts).

  • AD relevance: ischemia, not AD; no astrocytes.

  • Endothelial TER assay with thrombin‑cleaved SDC ectodomains

  • Model: HUVEC monolayers on ECIS; treatment with SDC3/SDC4 ectodomain ± MMP2/9/14.
  • Readouts: Decreased transendothelial resistance (TER) indicating hyperpermeability upon SDC ectodomain exposure (quantified by ECIS traces; experimental details provided) (PMID: 31091226; full‑text excerpts).

  • AD relevance: thrombin/inflammation axis; no astrocytes.

  • Glomerular endothelium; MMP‑mediated SDC4 shedding and permeability (mechanism‑transferable)

  • Model: In vivo/ ex vivo kidney; MMP‑2/9 inhibition restored endothelial glycocalyx depth and reduced albumin permeability/albuminuria; SDC4 localization to endothelium confirmed (PMID: 32037077; full‑text excerpts).
  • AD relevance: renal; no BBB or astrocytes.

Astrocyte‑only SDC4 perturbations (mechanistic, no barrier readouts)

  • Thy‑1→αvβ3 + SDC4 signaling in astrocytes activates PKCα→RhoA; SDC4 siRNA or tail truncation blocks focal adhesions/stress fibers (PMID: 19723805; full‑text excerpts).

Implication for the report

  • The absence of any published astrocyte–endothelial co‑/tri‑culture or BBB‑on‑chip study directly perturbing SDC4 with TEER/permeability readouts (under Aβ, cytokines, or VEGFA) remains a confirmed gap. Near‑misses show that:
  • Robust human BBB co‑/tri‑culture and chip systems with TEER/Papp under AD‑relevant inflammatory challenges exist (PMIDs: 37818458, 36978078, 40867058, 40524169), but none manipulate SDC4.
  • Endothelial‑only SDC4 perturbations do affect TEER/TER and permeability surrogates under ischemia/inflammation (PMIDs: 40571266/doi:10.1016/j.brainresbull.2025.111441; 31091226), supporting endothelial SDC4 as a barrier regulator.
  • Astrocyte SDC4 signaling is established (PMID: 19723805) but not linked causally to endothelial barrier function in co‑culture.
  • Conclusion unchanged: executing the predefined astrocyte‑targeted SDC4 perturbation in human BBB co‑/tri‑culture or chip models with TEER/permeability under AD‑relevant challenges is still the decisive experiment.

VEGFA–VE‑cadherin trafficking (endothelium‑only) — where to extract the numbers (PMID: 33596666)

  • Assay formats and conditions (full‑text excerpt level):
  • Antibody‑feeding VE‑cadherin internalization in primary ECs: anti‑VE‑cadherin (clone BV13) Alexa‑555 conjugate; 1 h at 4°C, unbound washed; VEGFA 30 ng/mL for 5 min at 37°C; surface signal removed by 25 mM glycine, 3% BSA, pH 2.7 acid wash; confocal quantification (PMID: 33596666).

  • Where the quantitative effect lives in figures:
  • VE‑cadherin internalization reduction in Sdc4−/− vs WT: Fig. 6D–E (PMID: 33596666).
  • VEGFA‑induced Evans Blue leakage reduction in Sdc4−/− vs WT: Fig. 6F–G (PMID: 33596666).
  • Directionality and significance (excerpt level):
  • “Primary Sdc4−/− ECs displayed reduced VE‑cadherin internalization following VEGFA compared to WT ECs” and “vascular leakage in response to VEGFA was significantly reduced in Sdc4−/− animals” (PMID: 33596666).

Medicinal Chemistry & Chemical Biology — follow-up 2 — full specialist report

Task: Question: Are there any high‑quality, vetted chemical probes for syntenin (SDCBP) PDZ domains that meet modern probe criteria and could be used in cellular models? - Query curated probe resources for SDCBP and close PDZ analogs; report probe names, target domain (PDZ1/PDZ2/dual), on‑target potency (sub‑µM preferred), selectivity data across PDZs/other proteins, cellular target engagement evidence, and recommended use concentrations. - State whether any meet accepted probe standards and highlight gaps. Tool to use: chemical_probes(SDCBP). · 1 PubMed call

Syntenin (SDCBP) PDZ chemical probes — none registered in curated portals; literature “tools” remain unvetted

  • Finding that changes the report: No high‑quality, independently vetted chemical probes are registered for syntenin/SDCBP in the Chemical Probes Portal or SGC probe collections (SDCBP; Ensembl: ENSG00000137575). This confirms there is currently no probe meeting modern portal criteria (characterised potency, selectivity, cellular target engagement, recommended use concentration) for the PDZ1, PDZ2 or dual PDZ sites of syntenin (Chemical Probes Portal/SGC query via chemical_probes tool: no entries for SDCBP).
  • Consequence: The syntenin PDZ inhibitors cited elsewhere in the report (e.g., PDZ1‑biased and dual‑PDZ small molecules) should be treated as research leads, not chemical probes. They lack independent probe‑portal vetting for PDZ‑family selectivity, in‑cell target engagement, and recommended concentrations; conclusions drawn from them in cells should be considered provisional until such data are generated.

What this means for cellular studies now
- No curated probe names to report; no portal‑vetted on‑target potencies, selectivity panels (across PDZs or broader proteome), cellular target‑engagement evidence, or recommended use concentrations exist for SDCBP PDZ domains (PDZ1/PDZ2/dual) (Chemical Probes Portal/SGC: no SDCBP entry).
- If literature syntenin PDZ inhibitors are used experimentally:
- Require fresh confirmation of direct binding to the intended PDZ domain(s) (PDZ1 vs PDZ2) and rank selectivity across a PDZome panel and unrelated proteins.
- Demonstrate cellular target engagement (e.g., CETSA/TPP, NanoBRET/SPR in cells, displacement of an SDC4‑EFYA tracer) and relate effects to occupancy.
- Use an orthogonal negative control (inactive analog) and a genetic control (SDCBP KO/PDZ‑mutant rescue) to ascribe on‑target biology.
- Avoid reporting a “recommended” concentration from this review — none is available from curated sources; derive working ranges from primary potency/engagement data with at least 10× safety margin to first off‑target.

Gap to close for a future, fit‑for‑purpose syntenin PDZ probe
- Biochemical potency: sub‑µM Kd/IC50 for the intended PDZ domain(s) (ideally <100 nM), with clear PDZ1 vs PDZ2 mapping.
- Selectivity: >30–100× over other human PDZ domains (PDZome) and clean liability profile in broad off‑target panels.
- Cellular target engagement: robust, quantitative engagement at ≤1 µM in relevant cells (e.g., NanoBRET/CETSA), with on‑target pathway modulation and inactive‑analog controls.
- In‑cell phenotypic window: recommended use concentration established where target engagement is confirmed and off‑targets are not engaged.
- Public documentation: full data package enabling independent assessment and registration with a curated probe resource.

Source
- Chemical probe resources query: no registered probes for SDCBP (ENSG00000137575) in Chemical Probes Portal or SGC (chemical_probes tool result — absence is definitive for these curated sets).

Medicinal Chemistry & Chemical Biology — follow-up 2 — full specialist report

Task: Question: What is the assay‑resolved potency landscape for small‑molecule and peptide ligands against syntenin (SDCBP) PDZ domains? - Use ChEMBL to retrieve all SDCBP PDZ‑targeted bioactivities; report compounds with Ki/Kd/IC50 values, assay type (binding vs functional/cellular), target domain (PDZ1, PDZ2, dual), and selectivity over other PDZs when available. - Summarize the best‑in‑class potencies and any structure–activity trends relevant to feasibility. Tool to use: chembl_bioactivity(SDCBP). · 1 PubMed call

New finding and impact on conclusions

ChEMBL records for syntenin (SDCBP) PDZ–targeted ligands show a clear split: dual‑PDZ (PDZ1–2) peptide/peptidomimetic constructs reach low‑nanomolar binding (Ki 30–40 nM), whereas the best reported small molecule binds full‑length syntenin at low‑micromolar affinity (Kd 1.8 µM). All activities captured here are binding (no cellular/functional IC50 in ChEMBL), and domain‑resolved selectivity (PDZ1 vs PDZ2 or off‑family PDZ selectivity) is not reported in these entries. This sharpens the report’s tractability judgment: peptide‑level disruption of the SDC4–syntenin PDZ interface is already in a nanomolar regime against the dual domain, while classical small molecules remain early (µM) and without documented PDZ‑family selectivity in these data (PMID: 33502198, 36939673).

- CHEMBL4785814 — Ki 100 µM [B]; dual (PDZ1–2) (PMID: 33502198).
- CHEMBL4779632 — Ki 110 µM [B]; dual (PDZ1–2) (PMID: 33502198).

Full‑length syntenin‑1 (contains PDZ1 and PDZ2; kinetic binding):
- CHEMBL5439384 — Kd 1.8 µM [B]; full‑length human syntenin‑1; kinetic binding assay (PMID: 36939673).

Selectivity information vs other PDZ domains or PDZ1‑only/PDZ2‑only constructs is not provided in these ChEMBL records.

Notes on physicochemical class (from ChEMBL properties):
- The 2021 nanomolar binders are high‑MW peptide/peptidomimetic constructs (MW ~1.3–2.5 kDa), consistent with dual‑PDZ engagement (PMID: 33502198).
- The 2023 small molecule (CHEMBL5439384) is MW 567, alogP 4.61, PSA 104 Å² (1 Ro5 violation) with Kd 1.8 µM on full‑length protein (PMID: 36939673).

Best‑in‑class potencies and SAR takeaways (from these records)

  • Best binding potency: Ki 30–40 nM achieved by dual‑PDZ1–2–targeting peptide/peptidomimetic ligands (CHEMBL4750927, CHEMBL4781637) in fluorescence‑based assays (PMID: 33502198).
  • Best small‑molecule potency: Kd 1.8 µM on full‑length syntenin‑1 (CHEMBL5439384) in a kinetic binding assay (PMID: 36939673).
  • SAR trends evident across the 2021 series (PMID: 33502198):
  • Increased molecular size and incorporation of multiple PDZ‑binding elements/auxiliary interactions correlate with sub‑100 nM affinity against the PDZ1–2 tandem, consistent with multivalent/avidity‑driven binding.
  • Shorter/simplified peptide constructs drop into low‑to‑mid µM Ki.
  • No domain‑selectivity (PDZ1 vs PDZ2) or PDZ‑family selectivity data are captured in these entries, leaving cross‑PDZ specificity unresolved in this dataset.

Citations: J Med Chem 2021 peptide/peptidomimetic PDZ1–2 series (PMID: 33502198); J Med Chem 2023 small‑molecule syntenin binder (PMID: 36939673).

Medicinal Chemistry & Chemical Biology — follow-up 2 — full specialist report

Task: Question: What curated on‑target safety liabilities and germline mouse knockout phenotypes are associated with SDC4 that bear on bleeding/angiogenesis, wound healing, fibrosis, and immune function risks? - Retrieve target safety annotations for SDC4, including human genetic/clinical associations if curated, mouse KO/phenotype summaries, and tissue expression‑linked risks; highlight findings relevant to systemic versus CNS‑directed modulation. - Provide a concise risk interpretation for antagonistic mechanisms. Tool to use: target_safety_profile(SDC4). · 1 PubMed call

New safety evidence for SDC4 and what it changes

What we found (Open Targets Safety): no curated human on‑target toxicology liabilities are recorded for SDC4, but the germline Sdc4−/− mouse shows multi‑system phenotypes spanning vasculature (retina and placental labyrinth), kidney, hematology/immune cell composition, and homeostasis. This strengthens the prior caution around anti‑angiogenic and inflammatory risks and adds renal and hematologic flags that were not explicitly itemized before, arguing for CNS‑restricted delivery for AD applications and specific safety monitoring in systemic programs (Open Targets Safety; Sdc4 mouse knockout phenotypes).

Curated items (germline Sdc4−/−; phenotype classes and representative terms)
- Angiogenesis/vascular development:
- Abnormal retina blood vessel morphology; abnormal placental labyrinth vasculature morphology (cardiovascular system, embryo, vision/eye) — developmental and tissue vascularization liabilities consistent with impaired angiogenic remodeling (Open Targets Safety; mouse phenotype classes).
- Hematology/hemostasis:
- Decreased mean platelet volume; increased red blood cell distribution width (hematopoietic system) — platelet size change could alter hemostasis, though no explicit bleeding phenotype is annotated (Open Targets Safety).
- Immune function:
- Decreased lymphocyte cell number; increased neutrophil cell number (immune and hematopoietic systems) — baseline skewing toward neutrophilia/relative lymphopenia (Open Targets Safety).
- Renal/urinary:
- Kidney degeneration; dilated renal tubule; abnormal inner medulla morphology — signals risk of renal injury with sustained systemic antagonism (Open Targets Safety).
- Systemic homeostasis/mortality under stress:
- Decreased circulating total protein/albumin; increased blood urea nitrogen; increased susceptibility to xenobiotic‑induced morbidity/mortality — suggests heightened sensitivity to physiological and drug‑induced stressors (Open Targets Safety).

No curated human clinical/toxicology liabilities were listed for SDC4; that is an evidence gap, not evidence of absence (Open Targets Safety).

Interpretation for antagonistic mechanisms (systemic vs CNS‑directed)
- Angiogenesis and wound healing
- Risk: High for systemic SDC4 antagonism. Developmental vasculature defects (retina/placenta) imply an on‑target role in endothelial remodeling; by extension, adult neo‑angiogenesis and wound repair could be blunted even if not explicitly curated here. Expect delayed wound healing and reduced pathological angiogenesis; ocular vascular effects are a specific concern. For CNS‑directed delivery, local vascular remodeling in brain could be dampened; balance desired BBB‑stabilization vs impaired vascular repair after stroke/trauma.
- Bleeding/hemostasis
- Risk: Indeterminate to moderate. Decreased mean platelet volume could impact platelet function; however, no bleeding phenotype is annotated. If developing systemic agents, include platelet function/bleeding time monitoring; for CNS‑restricted delivery the bleeding risk is likely lower but not zero for ocular tissues exposed systemically.
- Fibrosis
- Risk: Not directly supported by curated phenotypes. Renal degeneration can progress to fibrosis in many settings, but fibrosis was not specifically annotated here; treat as a hypothesis to monitor (renal biomarkers, imaging) rather than a known liability.
- Immune function/inflammation
- Risk: Moderate. Neutrophilia with lymphopenia suggests innate/adaptive balance shifts; systemic inhibition may modulate inflammatory responses to infection or injury. For CNS‑targeted approaches, peripheral immune impact should be reduced, but microglial/astroglial signaling cross‑talk may still change CNS inflammation.
- Renal function
- Risk: Moderate. KO evidence of kidney degeneration and tubular dilation argues for renal monitoring (eGFR, BUN, albumin) under systemic SDC4 blockade; CNS‑restricted delivery should mitigate this.

Systemic vs CNS‑restricted modulation takeaways
- Systemic SDC4 antagonism: prioritize non‑pregnant populations; monitor wound healing, ocular vasculature, platelets/bleeding, renal function, and leukocyte differentials; caution with combination anti‑angiogenics and nephrotoxins given xenobiotic sensitivity.
- CNS‑directed delivery (e.g., brain‑shuttled biologic, intrathecal): likely lowers peripheral renal/hematologic risks, but does not eliminate risks to ocular vasculature (from systemic exposure) or brain vascular remodeling; include ophthalmic monitoring and post‑injury vascular repair assessments.

Source: Open Targets Safety; Sdc4 mouse knockout phenotype classes and terms (no curated human toxicology liabilities recorded).

HPA single‑nucleus brain cell‑type metrics for SDC4 (what’s new and what it changes)

  • New: The HPA tool returns a single enriched brain cell type for SDC4 — astrocyte — with nCPM 253.3 and the classification “Cell type enriched | Detected in many.” No numeric “specificity score” or “rank across genes” is provided for brain single‑nuclei, and HPA does not list endothelial cells, pericytes, microglia, oligodendrocytes or neurons as enriched entries in this dataset. This confirms astrocytes rank highest in HPA’s brain single‑nucleus view; vascular endothelial and pericyte expression do not meet HPA’s enrichment threshold here. This does not change the report’s conclusions but formally documents the HPA metrics and their limits. Source: Human Protein Atlas, hpa_expression_specificity(SDC4).

  • Metrics returned by the HPA tool (brain single‑nucleus):

  • Enrichment category: Cell type enriched | Detected in many (HPA categorical specificity; not a statistical test).
  • Enriched entry and value: astrocyte nCPM 253.3.

  • Other requested cell types (endothelial cells, pericytes, microglia, oligodendrocytes, neurons): not listed as enriched; per‑cell‑type nCPM values and any “rank across genes” are not provided by the tool output in this run.

  • HPA metric definitions (as used here):

  • nCPM: normalized counts per million in healthy reference single‑nucleus profiles (not an AD vs control contrast).

  • Specificity classes are categorical (e.g., cell type enriched, group enriched, cell type enhanced, low cell‑type specificity) and indicate relative enrichment patterns rather than effect sizes or p‑values. “Detected in many” indicates broad detectability across brain cell types.

  • Concise comparative statement:

  • Astrocytes rank highest (only enriched brain cell type returned; nCPM 253.3).
  • Endothelial cells and pericytes are not enriched by HPA criteria in brain single‑nuclei (numerical levels and ranks not surfaced by the tool here), consistent with lower baseline expression relative to astrocytes in this reference dataset.

Source: Human Protein Atlas — hpa_expression_specificity(SDC4) tool output (Brain single‑nuclei cell type: “Cell type enriched | Detected in many; enriched in astrocyte 253.3”).

DrugCLIP predicted binders/pockets for SDC4 — none retrieved; tractability unchanged

  • What’s new: DrugCLIP/GenomeScreenDB returned no entry for SDC4 (gene symbol) or UniProt P31431 (AF-P31431-F1). This means no predicted pockets or small‑molecule binders are available from this screen for SDC4. This absence adds no new SBDD handle and leaves the report’s tractability conclusion unchanged (prioritize biologics and peptide/peptidomimetics; small‑molecule SBDD remains low‑probability).

  • Tool result: “No DrugCLIP target matched ‘SDC4’ …” and “No DrugCLIP target matched ‘P31431’.” GenomeScreenDB coverage is ~10,000 AlphaFold‑modelled human proteins; absent targets are typically unscreened because no high‑confidence domain passed the pLDDT/PAE filter (DrugCLIP, GenomeScreenDB; Jia et al., Science 2026).

  • Mapping (for context, since no pockets were predicted):

  • Experimental coverage (PDB): only the cytosolic tail/PDZ‑PBM is solved; residues 1–170 have no structure.
    • PDZ‑PBM complexes: 8BLV (1.50 Å), 1OBY (1.85 Å), 1YBO (2.30 Å); tail NMR: 1EJP, 1EJQ. Whole‑protein experimental coverage: 28/198 aa (14%); uncovered: 1–170 (pdb_structures).
  • AlphaFold confidence (AF‑P31431‑F1 v6; predicted):
    • Ectodomain 19–145: mean pLDDT 57.2 (low; flexible/disordered‑leaning).
    • Transmembrane helix 146–170: mean pLDDT 91.1 (high; well‑formed helix).
    • Cytosolic tail 171–198: mean pLDDT 63.2 (low; dynamic).
    • Model URL: https://alphafold.ebi.ac.uk/files/AF-P31431-F1-model_v6.pdb.

  • Credibility of would‑be pockets by region:

    • Ectodomain (19–145): low‑confidence AF and heavy HS/CS glycosylation (Ser39/61/63 HS; Ser95 CS) mean any AF‑defined pocket here would likely be a modeling artifact and sterically occluded in vivo (uniprot_protein_features; alphafold_model).
    • TM helix (146–170): high‑confidence but single‑pass helices rarely present solvent‑exposed small‑molecule pockets; no experimental TM structure (pdb_structures). A credible DrugCLIP pocket here would still be low‑ligandability.
    • Cytosolic tail (171–198): experimentally observed only as linear PBM bound in a PDZ groove (PDB above); the SDC4 tail itself is disordered/flat and not a small‑molecule pocket. Small‑molecule PDZ–PBM disruption is intrinsically challenging and is pursued via the partner PDZ groove rather than a SDC4‑tail pocket.

  • Conclusion for SDC4 SBDD:

  • With DrugCLIP returning no predicted pockets/binders for SDC4 and structural evidence showing no well‑ordered, ligandable SDC4 pocket, there is no new support for a small‑molecule, target‑directed SBDD path on SDC4 itself. The prior tractability view stands: pursue extracellular biologics and peptide/peptidomimetic disruption (e.g., SDC4–syntenin PDZ) while recognizing delivery constraints; small‑molecule SDC4 SBDD remains low‑probability.
Assay Development & HTS — follow-up 3 — full specialist report

What’s new and what it changes

Using pubchem_bioassays(SDCBP) I found 14 deposited PubChem BioAssay records that directly target syntenin‑1 (SDCBP), spanning PDZ1, PDZ2, the tandem PDZ1‑2, full‑length syntenin‑1, and one cell‑based syntenin assay. All 14 deposits are ChEMBL‑sourced method stubs tied to medicinal‑chemistry papers rather than standalone NIH/MLPCN HTS campaigns; none report Z′, plate statistics, or primary hit rates in the PubChem record, and counterscreens are not described at the AID level. This closes the gap by providing a concrete AID inventory and mapping constructs/readouts to the underlying publications. It also supports the report’s prior recommendation: use a fluorescence polarization (FP) or proximity assay (AlphaScreen/TR‑FRET) for primary screening against the PDZ–PBM interaction, with orthogonal biophysics (SPR/NMR) and cellular target‑engagement as confirmations. Where the deposits point to full‑length syntenin and cell‑based readouts, they are best used as follow‑up tiers rather than primaries (PMID: 33502198, 36939673; PubChem AIDs listed below).

Assay‑format implications, counterscreens, and artifact notes (from deposits + linked papers)

  • What the deposits do and do not contain
  • None of the 14 SDCBP AIDs include Z′, S/B, %CV, or primary hit rates. They are ChEMBL‑mirrored “result channels” tied to J Med Chem/Eur J Med Chem papers (PMID: 33502198, 36939673; doi:10.1016/j.ejmech.2021.113601).
  • Formats are not explicitly labeled in AID text; the 2021 peptide paper reports biophysical binding and X‑ray structures supporting PDZ1 targeting (PMID: 33502198). The 2023 small‑molecule paper reports binding to full‑length syntenin‑1 with orthogonal biophysics (PMID: 36939673).

  • No AID describes deposited counterscreens or frequent‑hitter filters; those must be built into any new HTS.

  • Recommended primary/orthogonal cascade for syntenin‑PDZ based on the AID landscape

  • Primary HTS (biochemical PPI disruption)
    • Fluorescence polarization (FP) competition: fluorescent SDC4/SDC2 PBM tracer vs syntenin PDZ1‑2 or individual PDZ domains. Throughput: primary screenable. Failure modes: fluorescent interference/inner‑filter effects; colloidal aggregation; compound fluorescence at tracer/emission wavelengths. Counterscreens: tracer‑only fluorescence, red‑shifted tracer variant, detergent (0.01% Triton X‑100) sensitivity, orthogonal readout below.
    • AlphaScreen proximity assay: His‑tagged PDZ1‑2 + biotinylated PBM peptide. Throughput: primary HTS. Failure modes: singlet‑oxygen quenchers and ROS generators, biotin scavengers, sticky colloids. Counterscreens: AlphaScreen TruHit controls (bead‑bead), dark/acceptor‑only controls, detergent sensitivity; confirm positives by FP or TR‑FRET.
    • TR‑FRET PBM binding: Tb‑anti‑tag donor on PDZ, FITC‑PBM acceptor. Throughput: primary/secondary. Failure modes: autofluorescent/chelating compounds; photobleaching. Counterscreens: donor‑only and acceptor‑only controls; metal chelator interference check.
  • Orthogonal biophysics
    • SPR with immobilized PDZ1‑2 or full‑length syntenin‑1 (as in J Med Chem 2023) (PMID: 36939673). Throughput: secondary/confirmation. Failure modes: nonspecific surface binding; DMSO mismatch; mass transport; aggregation. Counterscreens: parallel reference surface, detergent, concentration‑dependent kinetics.
    • NMR 15N‑HSQC on PDZ domains for chemical‑shift perturbations. Throughput: low; gold‑standard for binding site mapping. Failure modes: solubility/aggregation; slow exchange broadenings.

  • Cellular engagement/phenotype

    • Tagged‑protein proximity or imaging readouts akin to AID 1708568 (MCF7 co‑expression) (PMID: 33502198). Throughput: secondary. Failure modes: overexpression artifacts; cytotoxicity; optical interference. Counterscreens: unrelated PPI BRET/BiFC control; cytotoxicity; microscopy spectral controls.

  • Selectivity/counterscreen panels specific to PDZ PPIs

  • PDZ selectivity: parallel assays on unrelated PDZ domains (e.g., PSD‑95, GRIP1) to flag family‑wide binders (secondary).
  • Non‑PDZ counterscreen: a peptide–protein FP assay unrelated to PDZ to remove generic fluorescence/aggregation hitters.
  • Frequent‑hitter hygiene: add detergent; test redox reactivity (e.g., thiol‑reactivity), remove PAINS substructures; measure compound absorbance/emission overlap with tracer.

Bottom line on HTS robustness for SDCBP PDZ

  • The PubChem inventory confirms multiple deposited, target‑relevant assay channels against PDZ1, PDZ2, PDZ1‑2 and full‑length syntenin‑1 (AIDs 1708559–1708564, 1772477–1772479, 2029350, 2029353, 2029362, 2029363, 1708568), but none are full HTS deposits with Z′/hit‑rate statistics. The linked J Med Chem and Eur J Med Chem studies validate FP/proximity and biophysical confirmation as workable options rather than reporting campaign metrics (PMID: 33502198, 36939673; doi:10.1016/j.ejmech.2021.113601).
  • Most robust path for a new HTS:
  • FP or AlphaScreen/TR‑FRET PBM‑displacement as primary (explicit artifact controls).
  • SPR (full‑length syntenin‑1 or PDZ1‑2) and/or NMR as orthogonal binding confirmations.
  • A cell‑based syntenin proximity/engagement assay (AID 1708568‑like) as a later‑tier confirmation of intracellular engagement.
  • Because AID records lack Z′/hit‑rate data, those performance metrics will need to be established de novo during pilot mini‑sets (e.g., 1–5K compounds) before scaling. References to the underlying campaigns support feasibility but not quality statistics (PMID: 33502198, 36939673).
Assay Development & HTS — follow-up 3 — full specialist report

Task: Run consensus_search("syndecan-4 astrocyte endothelial co-culture TEER permeability BBB chip"). Identify any astrocyte–endothelial co‑culture/tri‑culture/microfluidic BBB studies that directly perturb SDC4 (genetic KD/KO/OE or pharmacologic/antibody) and report TEER/permeability outcomes. For any positive hits, extract cell sources, perturbation details, challenge conditions, quantitative effect sizes, and PMIDs/DOIs/preprint IDs. If none are found, state the negative explicitly. · 4 PubMed calls

Targeted BBB co-/tri-culture and BBB‑on‑chip search for direct SDC4 perturbations with TEER/permeability readouts

  • Finding and impact: A targeted full‑text search returned no astrocyte–endothelial co‑/tri‑culture or BBB‑on‑chip study that directly perturbs SDC4 (genetic KD/KO/OE or pharmacologic/antibody) and reports TEER and/or tracer permeability. This confirms the gap identified in the approved report; conclusions are unchanged.

  • Scope note: The only TEER/permeability studies with direct SDC4 manipulation remain endothelial‑only (no astrocyte co‑culture), e.g. hCMEC/D3/rBMVEC OGD/R with SDC4 interference improving TEER and tight junctions in vitro and Evans Blue in vivo (PMID: 40571266; Brain Res Bull 2025 doi:10.1016/j.brainresbull.2025.111441). Junctional trafficking work under VEGFA is also endothelial‑only and not a TEER/permeability assay (PMID: 33596666).

  • What was searched (and how): consensus_search("syndecan-4 astrocyte endothelial co-culture TEER permeability BBB chip") plus a narrower consensus_search("syndecan-4 knockdown astrocyte endothelial co-culture TEER permeability") and PubMed query syndecan-4[tiab] AND (blood-brain barrier OR BBB) AND (co-culture OR chip OR microfluidic OR TEER OR permeability). Titles/abstracts and full‑text excerpts (where available) were screened for SDC4 perturbation plus TEER/permeability endpoints.

  • Representative BBB co‑/tri‑culture and BBB‑on‑chip “near‑miss” papers (TEER/permeability present; no SDC4 perturbation reported):

  • BBB‑on‑chip with integrated micro‑TEER; human tri‑culture (endothelium, astrocytes, pericytes) and tracer permeability; no SDC4 manipulation (PMID: 36978078).
  • Micropatterned neurovascular interface; co‑culture TEER and Na‑fluorescein permeability under patterning; no SDC4 (PMID: 36139383).
  • Microfluidic BBB using iPSC‑BMECs co‑cultured with rat astrocytes; sustained high TEER; no SDC4 (PMID: 27399645).
  • All‑human 3D BBB with real‑time TEER; co‑/tri‑culture boosts TEER; no SDC4 (PMID: 28883042).
  • OrganoPlate TY10 human BBB‑on‑chip; co‑/tri‑culture, TEER and dextran leakage; no SDC4 (PMID: 38928202).
  • Dynamic BBB platform for TBI; co‑/tri‑culture, TEER and multi‑tracer permeability under cytokine/shear; no SDC4 (PMID: 37752338).
  • Human in vitro ischemia BBB model (hCMEC/D3 ± astrocytes/pericytes); TEER decline and FITC‑dextran permeability; no SDC4 (PMID: 32138745).
  • From inserts to chips: hiPSC‑ECs co‑cultured with 3D astrocytes; functional and transcriptomic shifts; barrier assays; no SDC4 (PMID: 40524169).
  • Tri‑culture protocols and comparative TEER/permeability across species/cell sources; no SDC4 (PMIDs: 28344002, 24262108, 33711041, 31167667; review/meta: PMID: 39304761).

  • Additional BBB co‑culture models with TEER/permeability but no SDC4: biomimetic GelMA co‑culture (PMID: 40332140), PEG‑hydrogel co‑culture (PMID: 39133897), JoVE microfluidic EC–astrocyte model (doi:10.3791/71652).

  • Outside‑scope positive (endothelial‑only SDC4 perturbation with barrier readouts, cited for completeness):

  • Endothelial HOXB9→SDC4 axis under OGD/R: SDC4 interference preserved TEER and ZO‑1/CLDN5/occludin; in vivo Evans Blue reduced (PMID: 40571266; Brain Res Bull 2025 doi:10.1016/j.brainresbull.2025.111441).

  • Bottom line: No astrocyte–endothelial co‑/tri‑culture or BBB‑on‑chip study was found that directly perturbs SDC4 and measures TEER/permeability. The absence is now re‑confirmed by targeted searches and should be treated as a real literature gap rather than a missed citation.

Medicinal Chemistry & Chemical Biology — follow-up 3 — full specialist report

Task: Using chembl_bioactivity(SDCBP), retrieve all syntenin‑1 (SDCBP) PDZ bioactivities. Identify and list: (a) sub‑µM small‑molecule actives (report molecule IDs, assay type/construct, measurement type and value with units), (b) the most potent dual‑PDZ peptides/peptidomimetics (Ki/Kd values), and (c) for top small molecules, basic physchem (MW, calculated logP, polar surface area) and any CNS‑tractability flags apparent from these descriptors. Conclude on the plausibility of CNS drug‑like small molecules in this series based on the retrieved set. · 1 PubMed call

ChEMBL SDCBP PDZ bioactivities — what’s new and what it changes

  • Retrieval confirms there are no sub‑µM small‑molecule binders of syntenin‑1 (SDCBP) PDZ domains in ChEMBL; the best small molecule is Kd 1.8 µM on full‑length syntenin‑1 (CHEMBL5439384) (PMID: 36939673). The most potent agents are dual‑PDZ peptides/peptidomimetics at 30–40 nM Ki (PMID: 33502198).
  • This tightens the report’s conclusion: CNS‑drug‑like small molecules for the syntenin PDZ groove have not yet emerged; the series is dominated by high‑MW peptides. The best small‑molecule physchem (MW 567, PSA 104 Å²) flags poor BBB permeability, reinforcing low near‑term plausibility for CNS‑orally tractable small molecules in this space.

(a) Sub‑µM small‑molecule actives against syntenin‑1 PDZ — none found

  • No sub‑µM small‑molecule activities are present in ChEMBL for SDCBP PDZ assays. The top small molecule is:
  • CHEMBL5439384 — assay type: binding (B); construct: full‑length human syntenin‑1 (N‑terminal GST‑tag), E. coli ER2566; measurement: Kd = 1.8 µM; method: binding affinity “assessed as dissociation …” (full text indicates direct binding on the full‑length protein) (PMID: 36939673).

(b) Most potent dual‑PDZ peptides/peptidomimetics (Ki/Kd, assay, construct)

All below were measured on the tandem PDZ1‑2 domain (“PDZ1‑2”) from E. coli BL21 (DE3):
- CHEMBL4750927 — Ki = 30 nM; assay: fluorescence‑based inhibition vs syntenin‑PDZ1‑2 (binding, B) (PMID: 33502198).
- CHEMBL4781637 — Ki = 40 nM; assay: fluorescence‑based inhibition vs syntenin‑PDZ1‑2 (B) (PMID: 33502198).
- CHEMBL4799812 — Ki = 170 nM; assay: SPR binding to syntenin‑PDZ1‑2 (B) (PMID: 33502198).
- CHEMBL4749490 — Ki = 250 nM; assay: fluorescence‑based inhibition vs syntenin‑PDZ1‑2 (B) (PMID: 33502198).
- CHEMBL4758599 — Ki = 250 nM; assay: fluorescence‑based inhibition vs syntenin‑PDZ1‑2 (B) (PMID: 33502198).
- CHEMBL4792861 — Ki = 250 nM; assay: fluorescence‑based inhibition vs syntenin‑PDZ1‑2 (B) (PMID: 33502198).
- CHEMBL4747962 — Ki = 250 nM; assay: fluorescence‑based inhibition vs syntenin‑PDZ1‑2 (B) (PMID: 33502198).

Notes: All entries above are peptide/peptidomimetic dual‑PDZ binders; measurements are binding (Ki) unless noted; one uses SPR (CHEMBL4799812). Source paper for this series: J Med Chem 2021 (PMID: 33502198).

(c) Top small‑molecule physchem and CNS‑tractability flags

  • CHEMBL5439384 — Kd = 1.8 µM on full‑length syntenin‑1 (B); MW 567.41; alogP 4.61; PSA 103.78 Å²; Ro5 violations: 1 (PMID: 36939673).
  • CNS flags from descriptors: MW > 500 and PSA ~104 Å² exceed typical CNS‑like ranges (often MW < 450–500; PSA ≤ 70–90 Å²), signaling low passive BBB permeability. While lipophilicity (alogP ~4.6) is within common CNS windows, the combination of high MW and PSA argues against CNS penetration without a shuttle/prodrug.

For comparison, other non‑peptidic entries in this ChEMBL set are larger, highly polar peptide‑like molecules (e.g., MW 690–944 with PSA 320–405 Å²) at 2–110 µM Ki — not CNS‑tractable by passive diffusion (PMID: 33502198).

Conclusion on CNS small‑molecule plausibility for syntenin‑PDZ

  • Based on the retrieved ChEMBL set, there are no sub‑µM small molecules for syntenin‑1 PDZ. The best small molecule (CHEMBL5439384, Kd 1.8 µM on full‑length syntenin‑1) carries MW 567 and PSA ~104 Å², both unfavorable for BBB penetration. The remainder of potent agents are peptide/peptidomimetics binding the PDZ1‑2 tandem at 30–250 nM, with very high MW and PSA incompatible with CNS exposure via passive diffusion (PMIDs: 33502198, 36939673).
  • Implication: CNS‑drug‑like small molecules for direct disruption of the syntenin PDZ groove are not yet in evidence; realizing this modality would likely require significant scaffold innovation to reduce MW/PSA while maintaining affinity, or alternative delivery (e.g., CNS shuttles) for peptide‑based binders.

  • Chemical Probes Portal/SGC query result: No registered chemical probes for SDCBP (ENSG00000137575) or SDC4 (ENSG00000124145). Consequently, there are no portal/SGC ratings, recommended cellular or in‑vivo concentrations, selectivity panels, or caveats available for either target via these registries (Chemical Probes Portal/SGC via chemical_probes tool).

What this changes: It confirms the report’s statement as a registry‑verified absence. Any small‑molecule or peptide agents used against syntenin‑1 PDZ domains or SDC4 in the literature remain non‑vetted research tools. Conclusions resting on them should be treated as provisional unless supported by original, target‑engagement and selectivity data.

  • Frequently used but non‑vetted tools: The registries do not list or flag any SDCBP/SDC4 tool compounds. Commonly cited syntenin‑PDZ agents in the literature (e.g., “PDZ1i,” dual‑PDZ peptides/peptidomimetics, and recent small‑molecule PDZ binders) therefore have no independent probe assessment and should be used with caution, with explicit orthogonal target‑engagement and PDZ‑family selectivity controls.

Critiques (ranked):

  1. [conclusion_changing] Assigned assay_development brief is not delivered; it is deferred to “Open questions.” You were asked to summarize specific BBB/astrocyte–endothelial in vitro models testing SDC4 with cell types, perturbations, readouts and effect sizes, and to recommend decision‑informative validation studies with concrete acceptance criteria. Instead, you write: “Direct, causal evidence in astrocyte–endothelial BBB models in an AD context is currently missing.” and then ask, “Does astrocyte‑specific SDC4 perturbation alter endothelial barrier properties (TEER, dextran/albumin permeability, CLDN5/OCLN/ZO‑1 levels) in co‑culture, 3D spheroid, or microfluidic BBB models? Search for ‘syndecan‑4 astrocyte endothelial coculture/microfluidic/spheroid BBB’…”. Deferring an assigned question is a defect; provide a structured summary of existing models (even if endothelial‑only), effect sizes, and a concrete validation plan with predefined acceptance criteria.
  2. [material] Unsupported genetics claim presented as definitive: “No IGAP AD GWAS locus flag for SDC4; although SDC4 has eQTLs, colocalisation with AD, stroke, white‑matter or cerebral small‑vessel disease GWAS was negative (0 traits with high H4 across ~105 SDC4 credible QTL sets).” There is no retrieved structured record or PMID supporting the specific ‘0 traits with high H4 across ~105 QTL sets’ statement. Either provide the colocalisation output as evidence (dataset or citation) or soften the claim to what is evidenced (e.g., ‘no AD locus in OT’).
  3. [material] Over‑specific Agora nomination interpretation without support: “Target nominations (Agora): Emory (AMP‑AD) and Emory‑Sage‑SGC (TREAT‑AD) nominate SDC4…; both predict antagonism (including inhibiting SDC4–SDCBP to reduce Aβ formation/release).” The retrieved Agora records list ‘2 nomination(s)’ and a risk score, but do not document the ‘predict antagonism’ or ‘reduce Aβ formation/release’ specifics. Cite the nomination text or analyses that make those predictions, or remove this level of detail.
  4. [material] Assay reporting lacks the requested effect sizes and full model specification. Examples are given (“SDC4 overexpression reduced TEER and tight‑junction proteins… SDC4 interference preserved them…”, “antithrombin reduced neutrophil adhesion… via an SDC4‑dependent mechanism”), but the assignment required, for each model, explicit cell sources, which cell was perturbed, perturbation modality (KD/KO/OE/Ab/peptide), and quantitative readouts with effect sizes. Provide these systematically where available; otherwise mark them as gaps.
  5. [minor] Executive summary buries the clinical biomarker lede: “...with supportive human transcriptomics and clinical biomarker associations...” without naming the specific study. The main text later cites “CSF SDC4 (Olink PEA)… (PMID: 42098850)”. Bring the specific CSF finding (direction and outcome associations) into the executive summary or cite the PMID there to inform decisions.
  6. [minor] You cite a PMID that was not retrieved this run in support of the ChEMBL fusion note: “ChEMBL ROS1 fusion (PMID: 27780853).” Either ensure this PMID is retrieved or drop the citation (the point—that these are ROS1 fusion assays and not SDC4 ligands—stands without it).
